Gonzalez2\nClark Barrett1\nYing Sheng1∗\n1 Stanford University\n2 UC Berkeley\n3 Shanghai Jiao Tong University\n4 Texas A&M University\n5 Independent Researcher\nAbstract\nLarge language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for complex tasks that\nrequire multiple generation calls, advanced prompting techniques, control flow, and\nstructured inputs/outputs. However, efficient systems are lacking for programming\nand executing these applications. We introduce SGLang, a system for efficient\nexecution of complex language model programs. SGLang consists of a frontend\nlanguage and a runtime. The frontend simplifies programming with primitives for\ngeneration and parallelism control. The runtime accelerates execution with novel\noptimizations like RadixAttention for KV cache reuse and compressed finite state\nmachines for faster structured output decoding. Experiments show that SGLang\nachieves up to 6.4× higher throughput compared to state-of-the-art inference\nsystems on various large language and multi-modal models on tasks including\nagent control, logical reasoning, few-shot learning benchmarks, JSON decoding,\nretrieval-augmented generation pipelines, and multi-turn chat. The code is publicly\navailable at https://github.com/sgl-project/sglang.\n1\nIntroduction\nRecent increases in the capabilities of LLMs have broadened their utility, enabling them to tackle a\nwider range of general tasks and act as autonomous agents [35, 6, 36, 52, 46]. In such applications,\nLLMs engage in multi-round planning, reasoning, and interaction with external environments. This\nis accomplished through tool usage [41, 38], multiple input modalities [47, 2], and a wide range of\nprompting techniques [30], like few-shot learning [5], self-consistency [53], skeleton-of-thought [33],\nand tree-of-thought [56]. All of these new use cases require multiple, often dependent, LLM\ngeneration calls, showing a trend of using multi-call structures to complete complex tasks [57, 21].\nThe emergence of these patterns signifies a shift in our interaction with LLMs, moving from simple\nchatting to a more sophisticated form of programmatic usage of LLMs, which means using a program\nto schedule and control the generation processes of LLMs. We refer to these programs as \"Language\nModel Programs\" (LM Programs) [4, 20]. The advanced prompting techniques and agentic workflow\nmentioned above fall within the scope of LM programs. There are two common properties of LM\nprograms: (1) LM programs typically contain multiple LLM calls interspersed with control flow.\nThis is needed to complete complex tasks and improve overall quality. (2) LM programs receive\nstructured inputs and produce structured outputs. This is needed to enable the composition of LM\nprograms and to integrate LM programs into existing software systems.\n∗Equal contribution.\nPreprint. Under review.\narXiv:2312.07104v2  [cs.AI]  6 Jun 2024\n\n\n--- PAGE 2 ---\nInterpreter\nSGLang Client (Frontend)\nLanguage primitives (Sec. 2)\nSGLang Runtime (Backend)\nOptimizations: RadixAttention (Sec. 3), Compressed finite \nstate machines (Sec. 4), API speculative execution (Sec. 5)\nFigure 1: System architecture: An interpreter executes language primitives with optimized runtime.\nDespite the widespread use of LM programs, current systems for expressing and executing them\nremain inefficient. We identify two primary challenges associated with the efficient use of LM pro-\ngrams: First, programming LM programs is tedious and difficult due to the non-deterministic nature of\nLLMs. Developing an LM program often requires extensive string manipulation, experimental tuning\nof prompts, brittle output parsing, handling multiple input modalities, and implementing parallelism\nmechanisms. This complexity significantly reduces the readability of even simple programs (Sec. 2).\nSecondly and importantly, executing LM programs is inefficient due to redundant computation\nand memory usage. State-of-the-art inference engines (e.g., vLLM [23], TGI [16], and TensorRT-\nLLM [34]), have been optimized to reduce latency and improve throughput without direct knowledge\nof the workload. This makes these systems general and robust but also results in significant ineffi-\nciencies for any given workload. A prominent example is the reuse of the Key-Value (KV) cache\n(Sec. 3). The KV cache consists of reusable intermediate tensors that are essential for generative\ninference. During typical batch executions of LM programs, numerous opportunities exist to reuse\nthe KV cache across multiple different LLM calls that share a common prefix. However, current\nsystems lack effective mechanisms to facilitate this reuse, resulting in unnecessary computations and\nwasted memory. Another example is constrained decoding for structured outputs (e.g., JSON mode),\nwhere the output of LLMs is restricted to follow specific grammatical rules defined by a regular\nexpression (Sec. 4). Under these constraints, multiple tokens can often be decoded once. However,\nexisting systems only decode one token at a time, leading to suboptimal decoding speeds.\nTo address these challenges, we present SGLang, a Structured Generation Language for LLMs. The\ncore idea is to systematically exploit the multi-call structure in LM programs for efficient execution.\nAs shown in Fig. 1, it has two parts: a front-end language and a back-end runtime. The front-end\nsimplifies the programming of LM programs, and the runtime accelerates their execution. The two\nparts can work together for better performance but can also function independently.\nWe introduce SGLang as a domain-specific language embedded in Python. It provides primitives\nfor generation (e.g., extend, gen, select) and parallelism control (e.g., fork, join). SGLang\nis compatible with Python’s control flow and libraries, so users can develop advanced prompting\nworkflows easily with native Python syntax. We provide an interpreter and a compiler for SGLang.\nThe interpreter manages the prompt state as a stream and submits primitive operations to the stream for\nasynchronous execution, ensuring proper control over synchronization and intra-program parallelism.\nAdditionally, SGLang program can be traced and compiled for more optimizations.\nOn the runtime side, we propose several novel optimizations to accelerate the execution of SGLang\nprograms. The first technique, RadixAttention, enables the automatic reuse of the KV cache across\nmultiple generation calls. In existing inference engines, the KV cache of a request is discarded\nafter processing is completed, preventing the KV cache from being reused across multiple calls and\nsignificantly slowing down the execution. Instead, our system maintains an LRU cache of the KV\ncache for all requests within a radix tree. This approach manages the KV cache as a traditional cache\nand uses a radix tree for efficient matching, insertion, and eviction. It allows the runtime to handle\nvarious reuse patterns with a cache-aware scheduling policy efficiently. The second technique is a\ncompressed finite state machine, which enables faster constrained decoding for structured outputs.\nExisting systems follow the constraints only for the next token by masking probabilities of disallowed\ntokens, making them able to decode only one token at a time. Instead, our system analyzes the\nconstraints and builds a compressed finite-state machine to represent the constraint. This approach\ncompresses a multi-token path into a single-step path whenever possible, allowing the decoding of\nmultiple tokens at once to achieve faster decoding speed. Lastly, SGLang also supports API-only\nmodels like OpenAI’s GPT-4, and we introduce the third technique, API speculative execution, to\noptimize multi-call programs for API-only models.\nUsing SGLang, we implemented various LLM applications, including agen\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 12000:28251]\npt state, generation, and\nparallelism. They can be used together with Python syntax and libraries. Here are the primitives:\n“gen” calls a model to generate and stores the results in a variable with the name specified in its first\nargument. It supports a “regex” argument to constrain the output to follow a grammar defined by a\nregular expression (e.g., a JSON schema). “select” calls a model to choose the highest probability\noption from a list. The operator “+=” or “extend” appends a string to the prompt. The operator\n“[variable_name]” fetches the results of a generation. “fork” creates parallel forks of the prompt\nstate. “join” rejoins the prompt state. “image” and “video” take in image and video inputs.\nExecution modes. The simplest way to execute an SGLang program is through an interpreter, where a\nprompt is treated as an asynchronous stream. Primitives like extend, gen, and select are submitted\nto the stream for asynchronous execution. These non-blocking calls allow Python code to continue\nrunning without waiting for the generation to finish. This is similar to launching CUDA kernels\nasynchronously. Each prompt is managed by a stream executor in a background thread, enabling\nintra-program parallelism. Fetching generation results will block until they are ready, ensuring correct\nsynchronization. Alternatively, SGLang programs can be compiled as computational graphs and\n3\n\n\n--- PAGE 4 ---\nTable 1: Comparison among LMQL, Guidance, and SGLang.\nSystem\nSyntax\nLanguage Primitives\nRuntime Backends\nLMQL\nCustom\nextend, gen, select\nHF Transformers, llama.cpp, OpenAI\nGuidance\nPython\nextend, gen, select, image\nHF Transformers, llama.cpp, OpenAI\nSGLang\nPython\nextend, gen, select, image, video, fork, join\nSGLang Runtime (SRT), OpenAI\nexecuted with a graph executor, allowing for more optimizations. This paper uses interpreter mode by\ndefault and discusses compiler mode results in Appendix D. SGLang supports open-weight models\nwith its own SGLang Runtime (SRT), as well as API models such as OpenAI and Anthropic models.\nComparison. Programming systems for LLMs can be classified as high-level (e.g., LangChain,\nDSPy) and low-level (e.g., LMQL, Guidance, SGLang). High-level systems provide predefined or\nauto-generated prompts, such as DSPy’s prompt optimizer. Low-level systems typically do not alter\nprompts but allow direct manipulation of prompts and primitives. SGLang is a low-level system\nsimilar to LMQL and Guidance. Table 1 compares their features. SGLang focuses more on runtime\nefficiency and comes with its own co-designed runtime, allowing for novel optimizations introduced\nlater. High-level languages (e.g., DSPy) can be compiled to low-level languages (e.g., SGLang). We\ndemonstrate the integration of SGLang as a backend in DSPy for better runtime efficiency in Sec. 6.\nRuntime optimizations. Fig. 2 shows three runtime optimization opportunities: KV cache reuse,\nfast constrained decoding, API speculative execution. We will discuss them in the following sections.\n3\nEfficient KV Cache Reuse with RadixAttention\nSGLang programs can chain multiple generation calls and create parallel copies with the \"fork\"\nprimitive. Additionally, different program instances often share some common parts (e.g., system\nprompts). These scenarios create many shared prompt prefixes during execution, leading to numerous\nopportunities for reusing the KV cache. During LLM inference, the KV cache stores intermediate\ntensors from the forward pass, reused for decoding future tokens. They are named after key-value\npairs in the self-attention mechanism [51]. KV cache computation depends only on prefix tokens.\nTherefore, requests with the same prompt prefix can reuse the KV cache, reducing redundant\ncomputation and memory usage. More background and some examples are provided in Appendix A.\nGiven the KV cache reuse opportunity, a key challenge in optimizing SGLang programs is reusing the\nKV cache across multiple calls and instances. While some systems explore certain KV cache reuse\ncases [23, 58, 18, 12], they often need manual configurations and cannot handle all reuse patterns\n(e.g., dynamic tree structures). Consequently, most state-of-the-art inference systems recompute the\nKV cache for each request. We will discuss their limitations and our differences in Sec. 7.\nThis section introduces RadixAttention, a novel technique for automatic and systematic KV cache\nreuse during runtime. Unlike existing systems that discard the KV cache after a generation request\nfinishes, our system retains the cache for prompts and generation results in a radix tree, enabling\nefficient prefix search, reuse, insertion, and eviction. We implement an LRU eviction policy and\na cache-aware scheduling policy to enhance the cache hit rate. RadixAttention is compatible with\ntechniques like continuous batching [60], paged attention [23], and tensor parallelism [44]. In\naddition, it introduces only negligible memory and time overhead when there is no cache hit.\nRadixAttention. A radix tree is a data structure that serves as a space-efficient alternative to a\nclassical trie (prefix tree). Unlike typical trees, the edges of a radix tree can be labeled not just\nwith single elements but also with sequences of elements of varying lengths, significantly enhancing\nefficiency. In our system, we utilize a radix tree to manage a mapping between sequences of tokens,\nand their corresponding KV cache tensors. These KV cache tensors are stored in a non-contiguous,\npaged layout, where the size of each page is equivalent to one token. Because GPU memory is quickly\nfilled by the KV cahce, we introduce a simple LRU eviction policy that evicts the least recently\nused leaf first. By evicting leaves first, we enable the re-use of their common ancestors until those\nancestors become leaves and are also evicted.\nIn the continuous batching setting, we cannot evict nodes used by the currently running batch.\nTherefore, each node maintains a reference counter indicating how many running requests are using\nit. A node is evictable if its reference counter is zero. Note that we do not preallocate a fixed-size\nmemory pool as a cache. Instead, we let the cached tokens and the currently running requests share\nthe same memory pool. Therefore, the system dynamically allocates memory for cache and running\nrequests. When enough waiting requests run, the system will evict all cached tokens in favor of a\nlarger batch size. Fig. 3 shows how the radix tree is maintained for several incoming requests. The\n4\n\n\n--- PAGE 5 ---\n(1)\nYou are a helpful assistant.\nUser: Hello!\nAssistant: Hi!\na\n(2)\nYou are a helpful assistant.\nUser: Hello!\nAssistant: Hi!\nb\nUser: Solve this problem …\nAssistant: Sure! …\n(3)\nc\nd\nYou are a helpful assistant.\nUser: Hello!\nAssistant: Hi!\nUser: What can you do?\nAssistant: I can ...\nUser: Solve this question…\nAssistant: Sure! …\n(4)\nX\nYou are a helpful assistant.\nUser: Hello!\nAssistant: Hi!\nUser: What can you do?\nAssistant: I can ...\n(5)\nevicted\nUser: Write a story …\nAssistant: Sure! …\nYou are a helpful assistant.\nUser: Hello!\nAssistant: Hi!\nUser: What can you do?\nAssistant: I can ...\n(6)\nUser: Write a story …\nAssistant: Sure! …\ne\nQuestion 1: …\nAnswer 1: …\nQuestion 2: ...\nAnswer 2:…\nQuestion 3: ..\nAnswer 3: ..\nf\ng\nYou are a helpful assistant.\nUser: Hello!\nAssistant: Hi!\nUser: What can you do?\nAssistant: I can ...\n(7)\nh\nUser: Write a story …\nAssistant: Sure! …\nQuestion 1: …\nAnswer 1: …\nQuestion 2: ...\nAnswer 2:…\nQuestion 3:\nWhat …\nAnswer 3: ...\nWhen ...\nAnswer 3: ...\nHow …\nAnswer 3: ...\nX\nYou are a helpful assistant.\nUser: Hello!\nAssistant: Hi!\n(8)\nX\nQuestion 1: …\nAnswer 1: …\nQuestion 2: ...\nAnswer 2:…\nQuestion 3:\nj\nWhat …\nAnswer 3: ...\nk\nWhen ...\nAnswer 3: ...\nHow …\nAnswer 3: ...\nl\ni\nUser: Solve this question…\nAssistant: Sure! …\nUser: How about ..?\nAssistant: It is a …\nevicted\nYou are a helpful assistant.\nUser: Hello!\nAssistant: Hi!\n(9)\nQuestion 1: …\nAnswer 1: …\nQuestion 2: ...\nAnswer 2:…\nQuestion 3:\nWhat …\nAnswer 3:\nX\nevicted\nX\nX\nevicted\nThis is ...\nLet us ...\nWe can ...\nTo solve …\nFigure 3: Examples of RadixAttention operations with an LRU eviction policy, illustrated across\nnine time points. The figure demonstrates the dynamic evolution of the radix tree in response to\nvarious requests. These requests include two chat sessions, a batch of few-shot learning inquiries,\nand a self-consistency sampling. Each tree edge carries a label denoting a substring or a sequence of\ntokens. The nodes are color-coded to reflect different states: green for newly added nodes, blue for\ncached nodes accessed during the time point, and red for nodes that have been evicted. In step (1),\nthe radix tree is initially empty. In step (2), the server processes an incoming user message \"Hello\"\nand responds with the LLM output \"Hi\". The system prompt \"You are a helpful assistant\", the user\nmessage \"Hello!\", and the LLM reply \"Hi!\" are consolidated into the tree as a single edge linked to a\nnew node. In step (3), a new prompt arrives and the server finds the prefix of the prompt (i.e., the first\nturn of the conversation) in the radix tree and reuses its KV cache. The new turn is appended to the\ntree as a new node. In step (4), a new chat session begins. The node “b” from (3) is split into two\nnodes to allow the two chat sessions to share the system prompt. In step (5), the second chat session\ncontinues. However, due to the memory limit, node \"c\" from (4) must be evicted. The new turn is\nappended after node \"d\" in (4). In step (6), the server receives a few-shot learning query, processes it,\nand inserts it into the tree. The root node is split because the new query does not share any prefix with\nexisting nodes. In step (7), the server receives a batch of additional few-shot learning queries. These\nqueries share the same set of few-shot examples, so we split node ’e’ from (6) to enable sharing. In\nstep (8), the server receives a new message from the first chat session. It evicts all nodes from the\nsecond chat session (node \"g\" and \"h\") as they are least recently used. In step (9), the server receives\na request to sample more answers for the questions in node \"j\" from (8), likely for self-consistency\nprompting. To make space for these requests, we evict node \"i\", \"k\", and \"l\" in (8).\nfrontend interpreter sends full prompts to the runtime, and the runtime performs prefix matching and\nreuse. The tree structure is stored on the CPU with negligible maintenance overhead. During the\nexecution of the fork primitive, the frontend sends the prefix first as a hint, ensuring the prefix is\ncorrectly inserted into the tree. It then sends the remaining prompts. This \"Frontend Hint\" simplifies\nruntime scheduling and matching, exemplifying the benefits of frontend-runtime co-design.\nCache-aware scheduling. We define the cache hit rate as number of cached prompt tokens\nnumber of prompt tokens\n. When there are\nmany requests in the waiting queue, the order in which they are executed can significantly impact\nthe cache hit rate. For example, if the request scheduler frequently switches between different,\nunrelated requests, it can lead to cache thrashing and a low hit rate. We design a cache-aware\nscheduling algorithm to increase the cache hit rate. In the batch-processing setting we sort the\n5\n\n\n--- PAGE 6 ---\nFSM state\nToken\nLLM decode\n(c) Decoding process with normal FSM\n(d) Decoding process with compressed FSM\n(a) Normal FSM for regex {\"summary\": \"\n1\n{\"summary\":  \"\n0\n(b) Compressed FSM for regex {\"summary\": \"\n{\"\nsummary\nLLM\n\":\nLLM\nLLM\n_\"\nLLM\nLLM\n{\"\nsummary\n\":\n_\"\n1\n2\n3\n5\n4\n6\n7\n8\n10\n9\n11\n{\n\"\ns\nu\nm\nm\na\nr\ny\n\"\n:\n0\n12\n13\n\"\n_\nFigure 4: The decoding process of normal and compressed FSMs (the underscore _ means a space).\nrequests by matched prefix length and prioritize requests with longer matched prefixes instead of\nusing a first-come, first-served schedule. Alg. 1 (Appendix) shows the pseudo-code for cache-aware\nscheduling with contiguous batching. The algorithm uses longest-shared-prefix-first order. In more\nlatency-sensitive settings we may still be able to tolerate limited batch re-ordering to improve cache\nreuse. Additionally, we prove the following theorem for optimal scheduling in the offline case. 2\nTheorem 3.1. For a batch of requests, we can achieve an optimal cache hit rate by visiting the radix\ntree of the requests in the depth-first search order, with a cache size ≥the maximum request length.\nThe longest-shared-prefix-first order is equivalent to a depth-first search order.\nThe proof is in Sec. A.3 (Appendix). In the online case, the DFS order will be disrupted, but\nour schedule still approximates the DFS behavior on the augmented part of the full radix tree, as\ndescribed in Sec. A.3. While greedy cache-aware scheduling can achieve high throughput, it can lead\nto starvation. We leave its integration with other fair scheduling methods [42] as future work.\nDistributed Cases. RadixAttention can be extended to multiple GPUs. For tensor parallelism, each\nGPU maintains a sharded KV cache. There is no need for additional synchronization because the tree\noperations are the same. Data parallelism with multiple workers is discussed in Sec. A.4 (Appendix).\n4\nEfficient Constrained Decoding with Compressed Finite State Machine\nIn LM programs, users often want to constrain the model’s output to follow specific formats, such\nas JSON schemas. This can improve controllability and robustness, and make the output easier to\nparse. SGLang offers a regex argument to enforce such constraints using regular expressions, which\nare expressive enough for many practical scenarios. Existing systems support this by converting a\nregular expression into a finite state machine (FSM) [54]. During decoding, they maintain the current\nFSM state, retrieve allowed tokens from the next states, and set the probability of invalid tokens to\nzero, decoding token by token. This token-by-token approach, however, is inefficient when there are\nopportunities to decode multiple tokens at once. For example, the constant sequence {\"summary\":\n\" in Fig. 2 spans multiple tokens in the normal decoding process as shown in Fig. 4 (c), requiring\nmultiple decoding stages, even though there is only one valid next token when decoding it. Therefore,\nthe whole sequence can be decoded in a single step (i.e., forward pass). However, existing systems\ncan only decode one token at a time because the lack of integration between the FSM and the model\nrunner in existing systems prevents multi-token processing, resulting in slow decoding.\nSGLang overcomes this limitation by creating a fast constrained decoding runtime with a compressed\nFSM. This runtime analyzes the FSM and compresses adjacent singular-transition edges in the FSM\ninto single edges as demonstrated in Fig. 4 (b), allowing it to recognize when multiple tokens can be\ndecoded together. In Fig. 4 (d), multiple tokens on the compressed transition edge can be decoded in\none forward pass, which greatly accelerates the decoding process. It is also general and applicable to\nall regular expressions. More details on the background and implementation are in Appendix B.\n5\nEfficient Endpoint Calling with API Speculative Execution\nThe previous sections introduced optimizations for open-weight models, which require modifications\nto the model inference process. Additionally, SGLang works with API-access-only models, such as\nOpenAI’s GPT-4. However, for these models, we can only call a black-box API endpoint.\nThis section introduces a new optimization for black-box API models that accelerates execution\nand reduces the API cost of multi-call SGLang programs using speculative execution. For ex-\nample, a program may ask the model to generate a description of a character with a multi-call\n2In practice, the computation is not the same as what is described in the proof of Theorem 3.1 because the\nunpredictable number of output tokens can cause the recomputation of the KV cache.\n6\n\n\n--- PAGE 7 ---\nMMLU\nReAct\nAgents\nGenerative\nAgents\nTree of\nThought\nSkeleton\nof Thought\nLLM Judge HellaSwag\nJSON\nDecoding\nMulti-Turn\nChat(short)\nMulti-Turn\n Chat(long)\nDSPy RAG\nPipeline\n0.0\n0.2\n0.5\n0.8\n1.0\nThroughput\n(Normalized)\nSGLang\nvLLM\nGuidance\nLMQL\nFigure 5: Normalized throughput on Llama-7B models. Higher is better.\nresult from KV cache reuse, the exploitation of parallelism within a single program, and faster\nconstrained decoding. Next, we explain the reasons for the speedup in each benchmark.\nOn MMLU, SGLang can reuse the KV cache of the 5-shot examples with RadixAttention. RadixAt-\ntention benefits both throughput and latency. RadixAttention reduces total memory usage by sharing\nthe KV cache, allowing for a larger batch size to improve maximum throughput. RadixAttention\nalso reduces the computation of prefill, thus decreasing the first token latency. On HellaSwag,\nSGLang reuses the KV cache of both few-shot examples and the common question prefix for multiple\nchoices, resulting in two-level sharing. For the ReAct and generative agents, SGLang reuses the\nKV cache of the agent template and previous calls. On Tree-of-thought and Skeleton-of-thought,\nSGLang parallelizes the generation calls within a single program and reuses the KV cache as much\nas possible. On JSON decoding, SGLang accelerates decoding by decoding multiple tokens at once\nwith a compressed finite state machine. In multi-turn chat, SGLang reuses the KV cache of the chat\nhistory. The speedup is more noticeable for short outputs because KV cache reuse mostly helps\nreduce the prefix time. For long outputs, because there is not much sharing between different chat\nsessions and the decoding time dominates, there is almost no speedup. In the DSPy RAG pipeline,\nSGLang reuses the KV cache of the common context example. On these benchmarks, the cache hit\nrate ranges from 50% to 99%. Fig. 13 (Appendix) lists the achieved and optimal cache hit rates for all\nof them, showing that our cache-aware scheduling approaches 96% of the optimal hit rate on average.\nWe exclude LMQL and Guidance from some of the last five benchmarks due to slow performance and\nmissing functionalities. LMQL’s issues stem from slow token-level processing and an unoptimized\nbackend, while Guidance lacks batching and parallelism support.\nResults on larger models with tensor parallelism. We run larger models, Mixtral-8x7B and Llama-\n70B, with tensor parallelism on the same set of benchmarks and report the results in Fig. 7 and Fig. 12\n(Appendix). The speedup on larger models shows a trend similar to that observed on smaller models,\nindicating that our optimization generalizes well to larger models. We omit Guidance and LMQL\nhere because they lack efficient implementations of tensor parallelism.\nResults on multi-modal models. SGLang has native support for multi-modal models with the\nimage and video primitives. The optimizations in this paper are compatible with multi-modal\nmodels. For RadixAttention, we compute the hash of the input images and use it as the key in the\nradix tree, allowing us to reuse the KV cache of the image tokens from the same image. We run\nLLaVA-v1.5-7B (image) on llava-bench-in-the-wild and LLaVA-NeXT-34B (video) on ActivityNet.\nBecause these models are not well supported by other baseline systems, we use the model authors’\noriginal implementation in Hugging Face Transformers as the baseline. As shown in Table 2, SGLang\nprovides throughput up to 6× higher on these benchmarks. In llava-bench-in-the-wild, there are\nmultiple questions about the same image, and SGLang runtime reuses the KV cache in this case.\nProduction deployment. SGLang has been deployed in Chatbot Arena [8] to serve open-weight\nmodels. Due to low traffic for some models, only one SGLang worker serves each. After one\nmonth, we observed a 52.4% RadixAttention cache hit rate for LLaVA-Next-34B [28] and 74.1% for\nVicuna-33B [7]. Cache hits come from common system messages, frequently reused example images,\nand multi-turn chat histories. This reduces first-token latency by an average of 1.7× for Vicuna-33B.\n8\n\n\n--- PAGE 9 ---\nTable 2: Throughput comparison on multi-modal LLaVA image and video models.\nModel\nLLaVA-v1.5-7B (image)\nLLaVA-NeXT-34B (video)\nAuthor’s original implementation\n0.18 image/s\n0.02 frame/s\nSGLang\n1.15 image/s\n0.10 frame/s\n0\n20\n40\n60\n80 100\nCache Hit Rate (%)\n20\n30\n40\n(a)\nBatch Size\nThroughput (tokens / s)\n0\n20\n40\n60\n80 100\nCache Hit Rate (%)\n200\n300\n400\n(b)\nTotal Latency (s)\nFirst Token Latency (s)\nLLM Judge\nTree of\nThought\nMMLU\nMulti-Turn\nChat(short)\n0.00\n0.25\n0.50\n0.75\n1.00\nThroughput\n(Normalized)\n(c)\nNo Cache\nNo Tree Structure\nFCFS Schedule\nRandom Schedule\nNo Frontend Parallelism\nNo Frontend Hint\nFull Optimization\n0.4k\n0.6k\n0.8k\n1.0k\n1.2k\n10\n20\nFigure 8: (a)(b) Cache hit rate ablation study. (c) RadixAttention ablation study.\nResults on API models. We test a prompt that extracts three fields from a Wikipedia page using\nOpenAI’s GPT-3.5 model. By using few-shot prompting, the accuracy of API speculative execution\nis high, and it reduces the cost of input tokens by about threefold due to the extraction of three fields.\n6.3\nAblation Study\nCache hit rate vs. latency/throughput. Fig. 8(a)(b) shows the relationship between cache hit\nrate and performance metrics (first token latency, total latency, batch size, and throughput) on the\ntree-of-thought benchmark. The figure is obtained by partially disabling matched tokens at runtime.\nIt shows that a higher cache hit rate leads to a larger batch size, higher throughput, and lower latency.\nEffectiveness of RadixAttention. We test the effectiveness of RadixAttention and its components\non several representative benchmarks. As shown in Fig. 8(c), \"No Cache\" means not using any\ncache, \"No Tree-Structure\" means using a simple table-based cache instead of a tree-structured cache,\n\"FCFS Schedule\" means using a first-come-first-serve policy instead of our cache-aware scheduling,\n\"Random Schedule\" means using a random order to schedule requests, \"No Frontend Parallelism\"\nmeans disabling parallelism in the interpreter, \"No Frontend Hint\" means disabling sending the\nfork hints from the interpreters, and \"Full optimizations\" means we turn on all optimizations. The\nexperimental results show that each of these components is required to achieve the best performance.\nDisabling parallelism and hints from the frontend interpreter also results in suboptimal runtime\nperformance, highlighting the importance of co-designing the frontend language and runtime.\nOverhead of RadixAttention. We test the overhead of RadixAttention on a benchmark without any\nKV cache reuse opportunities. The benchmark measures throughput on the ShareGPT dataset. It\ntakes 74.3 seconds to run 100 requests; however, the time used for managing the RadixAttention data\nstructures is only 0.2 seconds, which is a negligible overhead of less than 0.3%. This is because the\ncomplexity of tree operations is linear and small. Thus, we can turn on RadixAttention by default.\nEffectiveness of the compressed finite state machine. We test the effectiveness of the compressed\nfinite state machine and its components on the JSON decoding benchmark. Experimental results\nshow that the compressed finite state machine increases the throughput by 1.6× because it can decode\nmultiple tokens at once. In addition, we need to preprocess the state machine and reuse it for a batch\nof requests. Otherwise, redoing the preprocessing for each request makes the throughput 2.4× lower.\n7\nRelated Work\nVarious works have explored the reuse of the KV cache, and many of them are concurrent with our\nwork. Uniquely, our RadixAttention first proposes treating the KV cache as a tree-based LRU cache.\nIt is the first solution that supports multi-level sharing, cache-aware scheduling, frontend-runtime\nco-scheduling, and distributed cases. vLLM [23] and ChunkedAttention [58] explore some simple\nreuse cases (e.g., system prompt sharing) but do not cover multi-level tree-structured sharing or LRU\ncaching. PromptCache [12] proposes the modular reuse of the KV cache beyond the prefix but can\nimpact accuracy by up to a 43% drop. HydraGen [18], FlashInfer [59], and ChunkedAttention [58]\nfocus on CUDA kernel optimizations and do not include the concept of an LRU cache. API Serve [1]\nand LLM-SQL [29] study KV cache reuse for specific applications such as interleaving with external\nAPI calls and relational databases, but they do not have our radix tree or cache-aware scheduling.\n9\n\n\n--- PAGE 10 ---\nSeveral LLM programming and agent frameworks exist, such as Guidance [13], LMQL [4],\nDSPy [20], LangChain [24], AutoGen [55], and LLM Compiler [21]. Guidance and LMQL are most\nsimilar to SGLang, and we compare them in Sec. 2. Our innovation lies in novel runtime optimizations\nfor accelerating the proposed programming model. SGLang is compatible with other frameworks and\ncan accelerate them (e.g., the DSPy example in our evaluation). Additionally, SGLang is compatible\nwith many other common inference optimizations [60, 39, 3, 23, 59, 10, 26, 15, 19, 32, 31, 11].\n8\nFuture Directions and Conclusion\nFuture directions. Despite the progress made with SGLang, several limitations remain that reveal\npromising directions for future research. These include extending SGLang to support additional\noutput modalities, adapting RadixAttention to operate across multiple levels of the memory hierarchy\n(e.g., DRAM, Disk) [43], enabling fuzzy semantic matching within RadixAttention, providing higher-\nlevel primitives atop SGLang, fixing starvation in cache-aware scheduling [42], and enhancing the\nSGLang compiler to perform advanced static optimizations such as scheduling and memory planning.\nConclusion. We introduce SGLang, a framework for efficient programming and executing structured\nlanguage model programs. SGLang significantly improves the throughput and latency of complex\nLM programs through novel optimizations like RadixAttention, compressed finite state machines,\nand a language interpreter. It is a valuable tool for developing advanced prompting techniques and\nagent workflows. The source code is publicly available.\nAcknowledgement\nThis project is supported by the Stanford Center for Automated Reasoning and gifts from Astronomer,\nGoogle, IBM, Intel, Lacework, Microsoft, Mohamed Bin Zayed University of Artificial Intelligence,\nNexla, Samsung SDS, Uber, and VMware.
